4.3    Gaussian reference and optical covariance
This section constructs the Gaussian objects used by the weak block, Wick coordinates, replica forests,
and source connectors. Two objects must not be conflated:
1. the fixed tangent Feynman Gaussian on mean-zero lattice one-forms;
2. its exact conditional detail Gaussian for the linearized forty-nine-path Karcher block.
The first carries the massless retained field. The second is an optical, high-pass covariance with a uniform
gap. The nonlinear Wilson action, Haar and exponential-coordinate density, Karcher coarea factor, cutoffs,
inherited interaction, and source factors are not hidden in either covariance; they are exposed as exact
activities after the normal-fiber change of variables.

4.3.3 Exact Gaussian disintegration and harmonic extension
Let C≥j be the mean-zero Feynman covariance before the scale-j block and let Bj = 12 Af,j be the
normalized linearized retained map. On the mean-zero coarse space define

                                 Cjc = Bj C≥j Bj∗ ,        Jj = C≥j Bj∗ (Cjc )−1 ,

and
                                              Cjd = C≥j − Jj Cjc Jj∗ .
All inverses are taken only on the stated mean-zero spaces; torons remain in the retained compact
background.
Theorem 4.11 (Exact linear Gaussian fiber decomposition). The operators above satisfy

                           Bj Jj = I,        Bj Cjd = 0,       C≥j = Cjd + Jj Cjc Jj∗ .

If W ∼ N (0, Cjc ) and ζ ∼ N (0, Cjd ) are independent, then

                                                  A = Jj W + ζ

has covariance C≥j and Bj A = W . Conversely, this is the exact conditional disintegration of the fine
Feynman Gaussian given the retained linear block. In the folded Fourier frame, Cjd is the pushforward of
Copt from Corollary 4.10.
Proof. These are the finite-dimensional Gaussian Schur-complement identities. Since Cjc = Bj C≥j Bj∗ ,
Bj Jj = I. Substitution gives Bj Cjd = 0 and the covariance sum. The characteristic function of Jj W + ζ is
therefore the one with covariance C≥j . The folded optical parameterization T (k) identifies the conditional
detail space with ker Af (k), and the conditional precision is the restriction of Hf to that space.


                                                        28
  After canonical scale-two rescaling, define C≥j+1 to be the rescaled coarse covariance Cjc . Thus the
notation
                                          C≥j = Cj + C≥j+1
used below means the exact identity of Theorem 4.11, with Cj = Cjd and with the remaining covariance
embedded by Jj before the canonical identification. It is not a declaration of an unconstructed translation-
invariant product slicing.
Corollary 4.12 (Harmonic-extension Wick martingale). For every finite polynomial P in the fine tangent
field,
                           Eζ∼Cjd : P (Jj W + ζ) :C≥j =: (P ◦ Jj )(W ) :Cjc .
                                                       

The identity commutes with every fixed background mark and every prescribed finite family of passive
bounded source marks. The contraction operators commute and C≥j = Cjd + Jj Cjc Jj∗ . Therefore
                              1

                          e       j    e               P (Jj W + ·) 0 = e      j    (P ◦ Jj )(W ).

The covariance is fixed and independent of the background and source parameters, so those derivatives
commute with the contraction operators.
4.3.4 Exact Wilson–Haar factorization in the nonlinear fiber chart
Let PN (W, ζ) be the exact nonlinear normal-fiber chart proved in Sections 2.4– 3.2, and let Jcoarea be its
exact finite-volume coarea density. On the inner regular chart, write dγj (ζ) for the normalized Gaussian
with covariance Cjd .
Theorem 4.13 (Exact regular-reference identity). At finite volume and before any thermodynamic or
cutoff limit, the inner regular part of one Wilson fiber integral has the exact form
                Z                          Z              Z
                            −1
                   F (U )e−u SW (U ) dU = dW ρ0,j (W ) dγj (ζ) F (PN (W, ζ))e−Vj (W,ζ;u) .

Here ρ0,j owns the field-independent linear Jacobian and the empty support Gaussian normalization
exactly once, while
                                                             1

                                                             2

is defined with cj (W ) chosen to remove the empty-support term. Thus the identity is a change of measure,
not a Gaussian approximation. All nonquadratic Wilson terms, the complete field-dependent Haar and
coarea factors, normal-section corrections, inherited interactions, sources, and regular cutoffs remain
visible in Vj or in the charged irregular operators. No Gaussian product factorization across spatial
components is asserted.
Proof. Apply the exact finite-volume normal-fiber and coarea identity to the compact Wilson integral in
the boundary-rooted gauge. Multiply and divide by the strictly positive normalized density of γj . The
displayed formula then follows by the definition of Vj and the once-only normalization cj . The exact
partition into inner, curvature, collar, and outer labels is made before logarithms and is recombined
before reflection positivity is used. Gauge covariance, signed-hypercubic covariance, and aligned-reflection
compatibility follow from covariance of the Karcher map, its normal section, the tree gauge, and the
scalar Feynman precision. Individual artificial Gaussian detail measures are not claimed to be reflection
positive.

Gauge-covariant local jet projector. For a rooted support Y , choose the canonical root cell r(Y ).
Parallel-transport every local tensor to r(Y ) along the canonical hull tree and write the resulting local
field in the finite normal-coordinate atlas.
    Let JD (Y ) be the finite-dimensional space of gauge-complete, signed-hypercubic covariant, Wick-local
jets of declared engineering degree at most D, including the extraction sector and the stable jet sector.
Lemma 4.20 (Discrete covariant localization). There is a projector

                                          LocD,Y : FY 7−→ JD (Y )

with the following properties.
1. Loc2D,Y = LocD,Y , and it is exact on JD (Y ).
2. It commutes with gauge transformations, signed-hypercubic symmetries, and aligned reflection.
3. For 0 ≤ m ≤ 3,
                                ∥ LocD,Y FY ∥[m] ≤ CD (1 + τ (Y ))pD ∥FY ∥[m] .
4. If LocD,Y FY = 0, then after canonical scale-L rescaling,

                                 ∥TL FY ∥[m] ≤ CD (1 + τ (Y ))pD L3−D ∥FY ∥[m] .

  The estimate includes both the nonlocal output and its newly generated local projection:

                ∥ LocD TL FY ∥[m] + ∥(1 − LocD )TL FY ∥[m] ≤ CD (1 + τ (Y ))pD L3−D ∥FY ∥[m] .

Proof. On each finite local template, take the gauge-complete Taylor expansion in normal coordinates
and scale-dependent Wick monomials, transport all coefficients to the canonical root, and average over
the finite stabilizer. The dual coefficient functionals use finitely many derivatives and finite-difference


                                                     32
moments. Transport along a tree of radius τ (Y ) costs at most a polynomial in 1 + τ (Y ), proving the
third row.
   If LocD,Y FY = 0, every declared Taylor coefficient through degree D vanishes. The integral form of
the covariant Taylor remainder begins at degree D + 1. Canonical rescaling gives one root-preimage factor
L4 and L−(D+1) from the smallest omitted engineering degree. Thus the gain is

                                              L4−(D+1) = L3−D .

The regular linearized block kernel is analytic on the fixed tube and bounded through the three active
marked rows, so integration and the local coefficient functionals preserve this remainder estimate. Applying
the projector after integration proves the displayed localization-commutator bound; in particular, a
remainder cannot feed the finite local block without paying the same L3−D factor.

complete filtered low-jet matrix has been normalized.

Lemma 8.27 (Exact profile division). At every fixed field degree, the constant-profile vector kernel of the

                                               X

Proof. A zero-profile-momentum coefficient would be a local, reflection-compatible, gauge-invariant vector

zero trace; and two curvature two-forms cannot be contracted with invariant metric tensors while leaving
exactly one free vector index. The epsilon tensor has the wrong reflection parity. Hence the finite Laurent
polynomial Vµ (z) vanishes at z1 = · · · = z4 = 1. Successive telescoping in the variables zα writes it in the
ideal generated by zα − 1 and preserves finite range, proving (148).

Finite incidence identities The finite clover and incidence formulas satisfy:
1. exact cancellation of the distinguished-link derivative in the two-plaquette clover;
2. exact gauge covariance under arbitrary lattice G gauge transformations;
3. midpoint/reflection cancellation showing an O(a3 ) per-link strip defect;
4. exact signed-hypercubic covariance of the linear incidence stencil;
5. the zero trace of every first nonlinear adjoint diagonal block.
These identities, together with the all-stage marked theorem and the terminal joint Haar–optical connector
established in Part III, complete the finite-cutoff source construction.

Proposition 8.36 (Noncollapse and canonical normalization). The stress Schwinger distribution is

algebra, with exact translation contacts and signed-hypercubic covariance. The later angular Ward theorem
promotes its continuum multiplet to O(4) covariance.
8.2.5 Euclidean rotation covariance
Low-dimensional rotation-defect classification.
Lemma 8.38 (No low-dimensional gauge-invariant vector). For every compact simple G there is no
nonzero local, reflection-compatible, gauge-invariant vector source of engineering dimension at most four,
modulo vacuum terms and total derivatives.
Proof. A connection mass term is not gauge invariant. At dimension two, Tr dρ(Fµν ) = 0 by semisimplicity.
At dimension three, traces of dρ(DF ) reduce to derivatives of Tr dρ(F ) and vanish. A dimension-four
gauge-invariant vector cannot be formed from two curvature two-forms using only the metric while leaving
one free index; an epsilon tensor would have the wrong reflection parity. Total derivatives are excluded
by the stated quotient.


                                                         86
Lemma 8.39 (Antisymmetric stress defect begins at dimension six). A local signed-hypercubic, reflection-
even, gauge-invariant antisymmetric rank-two source has no component of engineering dimension four or
five. Its first possible nonzero continuum jet has dimension at least six.
Proof. At dimension four the quadratic curvature tensors are generated by Tr(Fµα Fνα ), δµν Tr F 2 , and
the corresponding dual-curvature contractions. The first is symmetric; the dual contraction is proportional
to a diagonal pseudoscalar tensor; none has an antisymmetric part. Reflection symmetry excludes the
odd-derivative dimension-five sector.
Corollary 8.40 (Vanishing rotation regulator). In the projective stress Ward identity, the antisymmetric
regulator insertion is a2 times a normalized family of dimension-six roots. The marked source bounds
therefore make it vanish in the zero-spacing limit. The cutoff rotation Ward identities integrate to full O(4)
covariance once the already established translation, reflection, and tensor-source continuity statements are
inserted.

Finite source multiplets and integration For every D < ∞, let J≤D be the finite-dimensional
renormalized curvature-jet source multiplet. The contact hierarchy preserves the engineering filtration.
On J≤D , δω is a finite matrix, allowing same-dimension composite-operator mixing.
Lemma 8.44 (Rotation algebra on finite source multiplets). The contact derivations obey

                                               [δω , δη ] = δ[ω,η]

on every J≤D and are compatible under D 7→ D + 2.
Proof. At finite cutoff the contact derivations are obtained by differentiating the geometric pullback of test
profiles, tensor indices and local source words. Geometric Lie derivatives obey the displayed commutator
identity exactly. Finite triangular source renormalization conjugates this finite-dimensional action and
therefore preserves the commutator. Dimension-raised regulator contacts vanish by Lemma 8.41; the
projective limit and the inclusions of source multiplets preserve the identity.
   Every finite-dimensional real representation of so(4) integrates to the simply connected group Spin(4).
The source multiplets consist of bosonic tensors built from curvature and covariant derivatives, so a 2π
rotation acts trivially; equivalently, their geometric tensor action factors through SO(4).
Theorem 8.45 (SO(4) covariance of the Schwinger family). Let ρD be the integrated finite-dimensional
action on J≤D . Then for every R ∈ SO(4),

                                  Sn (ρD (R)v1 , Rf1 ), . . . , (ρD (R)vn , Rfn )
                                                                                 

                                        = Sn (v1 , f1 ), . . . , (vn , fn ) .
                                                                           


Exact lattice reflection extends the covariance to O(4).
Proof. Along a smooth path R(s) in SO(4), differentiate the transformed Schwinger function. The
derivative is the left side of (165) with ω = R′ (s)R(s)−1 and therefore vanishes. Lemma 8.44 gives path
independence and the group law. Reflection is exact for the Wilson action, block map, clover source and
finite source normalization.



                                                       88
Osterwalder–Schrader continuation Let Dloc be the local field-polynomial test algebra. The O(4)
covariance proved above is an identity of the Euclidean Schwinger system. Rotations preserving the
chosen time-zero hyperplane act directly on the positive-time quotient; rotations mixing Euclidean time
with space need not preserve that quotient and are not asserted to act unitarily there.
Theorem 8.46 (Euclidean covariance and Poincaré reconstruction). The Schwinger family satisfies the
full O(4) covariance axiom. Together with reflection positivity, permutation symmetry, temperedness
and continuous translations, this gives by the Osterwalder–Schrader reconstruction theorem a strongly
continuous unitary representation of the proper orthochronous Poincaré group on the reconstructed Hilbert
space, with translation spectrum in the closed forward cone. Spatial rotations and translations are obtained
directly on the OS quotient; Lorentz boosts arise from analytic continuation of the Euclidean rotation
covariance.
Proof. Theorem 8.45 is precisely the Euclidean covariance identity required for every finite tensor/source
multiplet, and the nested source construction extends it to the complete chosen local field family. The
direct OS theorem, Lemma 8.14, and Lemma 8.13 supply the remaining reconstruction hypotheses,
including the linear-growth condition. The standard Osterwalder–Schrader covariance reconstruction
then produces the positive- energy Poincaré representation. No unitary action of a Euclidean rotation
that changes the reflection hyperplane is assumed on the intermediate OS quotient.

9     Continuum reconstruction and spectral gap
9.1    Osterwalder–Schrader reconstruction and clustering gap
The common Schwinger functional Let D be the countable algebra generated by positive-time
translates of the bounded positive-scale cylinders and the normalized finite local source fields constructed
by the marked hierarchy. The multiscale source-tree theorem supplies a projective Schwinger functional
S on D with the following properties:
(S1) temperedness and continuity in every Schwartz test tensor;
(S2) stationarity, gauge invariance, permutation symmetry, and reflection positivity;
(S3) continuous Euclidean translations;
(S4) for every two field polynomials F, G with separated supports,

                              |S(F G) − S(F )S(G)| ≤ CF,G e−m dist1 (supp F,supp G)                   (166)

     with one m > 0 independent of the chosen polynomials;
(S5) the OS linear-growth estimate of Lemma 8.13 on every finite source multiplet.
The constants CF,G may depend on the fixed fields and test functions; the exponent m does not.
Theorem 9.1 (Continuous OS reconstruction). Let Θ be time reflection and D+ the positive-time
subalgebra. The form
                                        ⟨F, G⟩OS = S(ΘF G)
is positive semidefinite. Its quotient and completion give a separable Hilbert space H, vacuum Ω = [1],
and a strongly continuous self-adjoint contraction semigroup

                                         T (t) = e−tH ,    H ≥ 0.

Proof. Reflection positivity gives the positive form and the null ideal N = {F : ∥F ∥OS = 0}. Positive-time
translations preserve D+ for sufficiently small translations on each compactly supported word and define
contractions on the quotient; the usual time-zero limiting argument extends them to all t ≥ 0. For a core

8.2.5 Euclidean rotation covariance
Low-dimensional rotation-defect classification.
Lemma 8.38 (No low-dimensional gauge-invariant vector). For every compact simple G there is no
nonzero local, reflection-compatible, gauge-invariant vector source of engineering dimension at most four,
modulo vacuum terms and total derivatives.
Proof. A connection mass term is not gauge invariant. At dimension two, Tr dρ(Fµν ) = 0 by semisimplicity.
At dimension three, traces of dρ(DF ) reduce to derivatives of Tr dρ(F ) and vanish. A dimension-four
gauge-invariant vector cannot be formed from two curvature two-forms using only the metric while leaving
one free index; an epsilon tensor would have the wrong reflection parity. Total derivatives are excluded
by the stated quotient.


                                                         86
Lemma 8.39 (Antisymmetric stress defect begins at dimension six). A local signed-hypercubic, reflection-
even, gauge-invariant antisymmetric rank-two source has no component of engineering dimension four or
five. Its first possible nonzero continuum jet has dimension at least six.
Proof. At dimension four the quadratic curvature tensors are generated by Tr(Fµα Fνα ), δµν Tr F 2 , and
the corresponding dual-curvature contractions. The first is symmetric; the dual contraction is proportional
to a diagonal pseudoscalar tensor; none has an antisymmetric part. Reflection symmetry excludes the
odd-derivative dimension-five sector.
Corollary 8.40 (Vanishing rotation regulator). In the projective stress Ward identity, the antisymmetric
regulator insertion is a2 times a normalized family of dimension-six roots. The marked source bounds
therefore make it vanish in the zero-spacing limit. The cutoff rotation Ward identities integrate to full O(4)
covariance once the already established translation, reflection, and tensor-source continuity statements are
inserted.

Finite source multiplets and integration For every D < ∞, let J≤D be the finite-dimensional

                                               [δω , δη ] = δ[ω,η]

Proof. At finite cutoff the contact derivations are obtained by differentiating the geometric pullback of test

therefore preserves the commutator. Dimension-raised regulator contacts vanish by Lemma 8.41; the

   Every finite-dimensional real representation of so(4) integrates to the simply connected group Spin(4).

Osterwalder–Schrader continuation Let Dloc be the local field-polynomial test algebra. The O(4)
covariance proved above is an identity of the Euclidean Schwinger system. Rotations preserving the
chosen time-zero hyperplane act directly on the positive-time quotient; rotations mixing Euclidean time
with space need not preserve that quotient and are not asserted to act unitarily there.
Theorem 8.46 (Euclidean covariance and Poincaré reconstruction). The Schwinger family satisfies the
full O(4) covariance axiom. Together with reflection positivity, permutation symmetry, temperedness
and continuous translations, this gives by the Osterwalder–Schrader reconstruction theorem a strongly
continuous unitary representation of the proper orthochronous Poincaré group on the reconstructed Hilbert
space, with translation spectrum in the closed forward cone. Spatial rotations and translations are obtained
directly on the OS quotient; Lorentz boosts arise from analytic continuation of the Euclidean rotation
covariance.
Proof. Theorem 8.45 is precisely the Euclidean covariance identity required for every finite tensor/source
multiplet, and the nested source construction extends it to the complete chosen local field family. The
direct OS theorem, Lemma 8.14, and Lemma 8.13 supply the remaining reconstruction hypotheses,
including the linear-growth condition. The standard Osterwalder–Schrader covariance reconstruction
then produces the positive- energy Poincaré representation. No unitary action of a Euclidean rotation
that changes the reflection hyperplane is assumed on the intermediate OS quotient.

9     Continuum reconstruction and spectral gap
9.1    Osterwalder–Schrader reconstruction and clustering gap
The common Schwinger functional Let D be the countable algebra generated by positive-time
translates of the bounded positive-scale cylinders and the normalized finite local source fields constructed
by the marked hierarchy. The multiscale source-tree theorem supplies a projective Schwinger functional
S on D with the following properties:
(S1) temperedness and continuity in every Schwartz test tensor;
(S2) stationarity, gauge invariance, permutation symmetry, and reflection positivity;
(S3) continuous Euclidean translations;
(S4) for every two field polynomials F, G with separated supports,

                              |S(F G) − S(F )S(G)| ≤ CF,G e−m dist1 (supp F,supp G)                   (166)

     with one m > 0 independent of the chosen polynomials;
(S5) the OS linear-growth estimate of Lemma 8.13 on every finite source multiplet.
The constants CF,G may depend on the fixed fields and test functions; the exponent m does not.
Theorem 9.1 (Continuous OS reconstruction). Let Θ be time reflection and D+ the positive-time
subalgebra. The form
                                        ⟨F, G⟩OS = S(ΘF G)
is positive semidefinite. Its quotient and completion give a separable Hilbert space H, vacuum Ω = [1],
and a strongly continuous self-adjoint contraction semigroup

                                         T (t) = e−tH ,    H ≥ 0.

Proof. Reflection positivity gives the positive form and the null ideal N = {F : ∥F ∥OS = 0}. Positive-time
translations preserve D+ for sufficiently small translations on each compactly supported word and define
contractions on the quotient; the usual time-zero limiting argument extends them to all t ≥ 0. For a core
vector [F ],
                                        2
                      ∥T (t)[F ] − [F ]∥ = S(ΘF τ2t F ) − 2ℜS(ΘF τt F ) + S(ΘF F ),
which tends to zero by translation continuity. Contractivity and density give strong continuity on H.
Reflection symmetry makes the semigroup self-adjoint, and the spectral theorem gives T (t) = e−tH with
H ≥ 0.

Spectral consequence of one common clustering rate Let D+,0 be the centered positive-time
core,
                              D+,0 = {F ∈ D+ : S(F ) = 0}.
For F ∈ D+,0 let µF be the positive spectral measure of H in the vector [F ].


                                                     89
Lemma 9.2 (Core spectral support). For every F ∈ D+,0 ,

                                              supp µF ⊂ [m, ∞).                                       (167)

Proof. Let the time support of F lie in a fixed compact interval. For all sufficiently large t, the supports

                               Z ∞
                          0≤        e−tλ dµF (λ) = ⟨[F ], T (t)[F ]⟩ ≤ CF e−mt .                       (168)
                                  0

                                      e−(m−ε)t µF ([0, m − ε]) ≤ CF e−mt .

Theorem 9.3 (Unique vacuum and complete local mass gap). The OS Hamiltonian satisfies

                                               H Ω⊥ ≥ m > 0.                                          (169)

The vacuum is unique.
Proof. The centered classes [F ] − S(F )Ω, F ∈ D+ , are dense in Ω⊥ . Lemma 9.2 says that the spectral
projection EH ([0, m − ε]) annihilates this dense set for every ε > 0; hence it vanishes on Ω⊥ . This proves
the absence of spectrum in (0, m).
   For a centered core vector, (168) tends to zero, so its spectral measure has no atom at zero. Density
then shows that EH ({0})H = CΩ. Thus the vacuum is unique and (169) holds.

10     Nontriviality and finite spectral window
10.1     Endpoint interpolation and reflected noncollapse
Let Yr (f ) be the tangent-normalized, Wick-centered scalar curvature source at physical scale r, with the
source root parallel transported in the normalized finite-jet frame. The test profile f is chosen once at
unit scale and dilated with the source so that its dimensionless support remains fixed and lies strictly in
positive Euclidean time.
Lemma 10.1 (Positive Gaussian OS norm). There is such a profile f for which

                                    V0 (f ) := S0 ΘYr (f ) Yr (f ) > 0.
                                                                  


The value is independent of r in the normalized frame.
Proof. At the Gaussian fixed point the centered scalar curvature composite creates a two-curvature Fock
vector. In Fourier variables its squared norm is an integral of the squared symmetrized two-particle
amplitude against the positive transverse curvature spectral density. That density is nonzero on an open
set, so choosing a positive-time profile whose Fourier transform is nonzero there gives a strictly positive
integral. Reflection positivity identifies the integral with the displayed OS form. Scale covariance of the
tangent-normalized source makes the value independent of r.

10.2     Uniform endpoint interpolation
Theorem 10.2 (Uniform endpoint interpolation master theorem). Fix the positive-time profile f and a
                                                   √
physical weak scale r, put ur = u(r) and gr = ur , and use g rather than u = g 2 as the interpolation
parameter. For the complete finite-volume normalized reflected two-root functional, and also with one
fixed finite defect root, the following hold on the full segment 0 ≤ g ≤ gr :
    (i) the graph-segment identities are C 2 and exact before every limit;
   (ii) the stable/wrapping orbit rows, the pre-owner two-mark compact entrance row, and the terminal
        packet-shell row have constants independent of cutoff, volume, preceding weak depth, crossing cell,
        and terminal depth M ;

Theorem 10.4 (Endpoint packet-shell supplier). For the graph-segment family constructed above, the
complete twice-marked endpoint expansion has the following properties.
    (i) The grounded periodic covariance has volume-independent AA, AF , and F F rows of orders two,
        three, and four. After the exact global contact is subtracted, every complete reflection/BRS packet
        has two lattice-difference factors and therefore gains (r/ℓi )2 at weak scale i.
   (ii) The exact weak recurrence admits a finite crossing-cell decomposition. On each fixed cell, both g
        derivatives are taken before the labelled large-field partition and before bad-core ownership. The
        differentiated partition is then recollected with the same charged events and with no same-owner
        cancellation assumption.
 (iii) Before crossing, the full rooted stable map, including wrapping and charged-flat coordinates, is C 2
        in g with a strictly contracting same-order marked row. At first crossing, the fresh marked family
        lies in a uniform hybrid pivot–jet polydisc and satisfies the original-link Fourier–Wiener incidence
        row required by the joint Haar–optical collect.
  (iv) The crossing contribution and the no-crossing terminal contribution are controlled by one geometri-
        cally summable conditional-packet shell majorant. The same majorant controls the functional and
        its first two g derivatives, extends to the closed crossing cells, and sends the terminal remainder to
        zero uniformly as the terminal depth tends to infinity.
Consequently the complete normalized two-root functional, with or without one fixed dimension-six defect
root, has a cutoff-, volume-, crossing-cell-, and terminal-depth-independent C 2 bound on 0 ≤ g ≤ gr .
Proof. Appendix A gives the full proof inside this manuscript. Its five subsections establish the grounded-
torus covariance and globally subtracted packet division; the weak crossing recurrence and wrapping rows;
the twice-marked conditional-packet contraction; the pre-owner hybrid-polydisc Wiener and compact-
entrance estimates; and the terminal-shell summation. No additional mathematical input is imported
beyond the results proved in Sections 3–7.

11     Completion for arbitrary fixed compact simple gauge groups
Theorem 11.1 (Completion of the fixed-group construction). For every fixed compact connected simple
G and faithful unitary representation ρ, the group register CG is finite and all hypotheses consumed by
Sections 2–10 are satisfied. Consequently Theorem 1.1 holds.
Proof. Compactness and the analytic bi-invariant geometry give the Karcher, logarithm, coarea, and
faithful-action constants of Section 2. The complete folded block symbol is spacetime-only and tensors
with Ig , so the strict right-inverse and optical coercivity constants are unchanged. The chart complement
is charged by the faithful Wilson action and the irregular activity is exponentially small. The permanent
weak map, centered graph, and BPHZL packet use the same finite-dimensional Lie-algebra tensor calculus,
with the universal color factor CA (G).
   The Peter–Weyl spectral sum converges at the fixed order sG , and the higher hybrid-polydisc argument
supplies the one-pivot nuclear and multipivot Fourier–Wiener rows. The ordinary-periodic toron, seam,
reflection, and wrapping templates are admitted by Theorem D.3; no twisted sector or finite-volume bundle
sum is used. The invariant-jet theorem supplies the complete low-dimensional mixing and dimension-six
regulator spaces. The joint Haar–optical forest, source transport, Ward hierarchy, OS reconstruction,
and noncollapse theorem then apply with constants depending on G but uniform in cutoff, volume, and
directed depth. Appendix F, specifically Table 3, records every independent entry of CG , the first proof
call at which its G-dependence enters, and the subsequent consumers. Thus no additional estimate
uniform in G is hidden in this completion step: after those finite calls, each remaining mechanism is
color-free, tensors with Ig , or takes a finite maximum in a registered finite-dimensional space.
Corollary 11.2 (The SU(N ) family). For every fixed integer N ≥ 2, the theorem holds for G = SU(N )
in the defining representation, and the scale-two map is
                                               11N log 2
                             u(2L) = u(L) +              u(L)2 + ON (u(L)3 ).

No estimate uniform as N → ∞ is asserted.
Remark 11.3 (Logical scope). The construction is a fixed-group theorem. The directed subsequence,
curvature-jet observable scope, and absence of a large-rank uniformity claim are part of the statement,
not omitted conclusions.

Proposition 8.36 (Noncollapse and canonical normalization). The stress Schwinger distribution is

distributional translation derivative is nonzero for suitable separated test profiles. Choose v supported
near one insertion. The right side of (160) is then nonzero, so the tensor insertion on the left cannot
vanish. Multiplying the tensor by a finite constant would multiply the left contact coefficient but not the
geometric derivation on the right, forcing the constant to be one.

Result and local-field realization
Theorem 8.37 (Stress/Ward construction). The global displacement theorem plus the projective construc-
tion above produce a nonzero tempered Euclidean tensor insertion on the bounded geometric Schwinger
algebra, with exact translation contacts and signed-hypercubic covariance. The later angular Ward theorem
promotes its continuum multiplet to O(4) covariance.
8.2.5 Euclidean rotation covariance
Low-dimensional rotation-defect classification.
Lemma 8.38 (No low-dimensional gauge-invariant vector). For every compact simple G there is no
nonzero local, reflection-compatible, gauge-invariant vector source of engineering dimension at most four,
modulo vacuum terms and total derivatives.
Proof. A connection mass term is not gauge invariant. At dimension two, Tr dρ(Fµν ) = 0 by semisimplicity.
At dimension three, traces of dρ(DF ) reduce to derivatives of Tr dρ(F ) and vanish. A dimension-four
gauge-invariant vector cannot be formed from two curvature two-forms using only the metric while leaving
one free index; an epsilon tensor would have the wrong reflection parity. Total derivatives are excluded
by the stated quotient.


                                                         86
Lemma 8.39 (Antisymmetric stress defect begins at dimension six). A local signed-hypercubic, reflection-
even, gauge-invariant antisymmetric rank-two source has no component of engineering dimension four or
five. Its first possible nonzero continuum jet has dimension at least six.
Proof. At dimension four the quadratic curvature tensors are generated by Tr(Fµα Fνα ), δµν Tr F 2 , and
the corresponding dual-curvature contractions. The first is symmetric; the dual contraction is proportional
to a diagonal pseudoscalar tensor; none has an antisymmetric part. Reflection symmetry excludes the
odd-derivative dimension-five sector.
Corollary 8.40 (Vanishing rotation regulator). In the projective stress Ward identity, the antisymmetric
regulator insertion is a2 times a normalized family of dimension-six roots. The marked source bounds
therefore make it vanish in the zero-spacing limit. The cutoff rotation Ward identities integrate to full O(4)
covariance once the already established translation, reflection, and tensor-source continuity statements are
inserted.

The symmetric strain and divergence of vR are supported in the annulus R ≤ |x| ≤ 2R and are uniformly
bounded in the corresponding scaled Schwartz seminorms.

Proof. The stress and scalar source roots are centered by their vacuum rows. Their supports lie in the

Finite source multiplets and integration For every D < ∞, let J≤D be the finite-dimensional
renormalized curvature-jet source multiplet. The contact hierarchy preserves the engineering filtration.
On J≤D , δω is a finite matrix, allowing same-dimension composite-operator mixing.
Lemma 8.44 (Rotation algebra on finite source multiplets). The contact derivations obey

                                               [δω , δη ] = δ[ω,η]

on every J≤D and are compatible under D 7→ D + 2.
Proof. At finite cutoff the contact derivations are obtained by differentiating the geometric pullback of test
profiles, tensor indices and local source words. Geometric Lie derivatives obey the displayed commutator
identity exactly. Finite triangular source renormalization conjugates this finite-dimensional action and
therefore preserves the commutator. Dimension-raised regulator contacts vanish by Lemma 8.41; the
projective limit and the inclusions of source multiplets preserve the identity.
   Every finite-dimensional real representation of so(4) integrates to the simply connected group Spin(4).
The source multiplets consist of bosonic tensors built from curvature and covariant derivatives, so a 2π
rotation acts trivially; equivalently, their geometric tensor action factors through SO(4).
Theorem 8.45 (SO(4) covariance of the Schwinger family). Let ρD be the integrated finite-dimensional
action on J≤D . Then for every R ∈ SO(4),

                                  Sn (ρD (R)v1 , Rf1 ), . . . , (ρD (R)vn , Rfn )
                                                                                 

                                        = Sn (v1 , f1 ), . . . , (vn , fn ) .
                                                                           


Exact lattice reflection extends the covariance to O(4).
Proof. Along a smooth path R(s) in SO(4), differentiate the transformed Schwinger function. The
derivative is the left side of (165) with ω = R′ (s)R(s)−1 and therefore vanishes. Lemma 8.44 gives path
independence and the group law. Reflection is exact for the Wilson action, block map, clover source and
finite source normalization.



                                                       88
Osterwalder–Schrader continuation Let Dloc be the local field-polynomial test algebra. The O(4)
covariance proved above is an identity of the Euclidean Schwinger system. Rotations preserving the
chosen time-zero hyperplane act directly on the positive-time quotient; rotations mixing Euclidean time
with space need not preserve that quotient and are not asserted to act unitarily there.
Theorem 8.46 (Euclidean covariance and Poincaré reconstruction). The Schwinger family satisfies the
full O(4) covariance axiom. Together with reflection positivity, permutation symmetry, temperedness
and continuous translations, this gives by the Osterwalder–Schrader reconstruction theorem a strongly
continuous unitary representation of the proper orthochronous Poincaré group on the reconstructed Hilbert
space, with translation spectrum in the closed forward cone. Spatial rotations and translations are obtained
directly on the OS quotient; Lorentz boosts arise from analytic continuation of the Euclidean rotation
covariance.
Proof. Theorem 8.45 is precisely the Euclidean covariance identity required for every finite tensor/source
multiplet, and the nested source construction extends it to the complete chosen local field family. The
direct OS theorem, Lemma 8.14, and Lemma 8.13 supply the remaining reconstruction hypotheses,
including the linear-growth condition. The standard Osterwalder–Schrader covariance reconstruction
then produces the positive- energy Poincaré representation. No unitary action of a Euclidean rotation
that changes the reflection hyperplane is assumed on the intermediate OS quotient.

9     Continuum reconstruction and spectral gap
9.1    Osterwalder–Schrader reconstruction and clustering gap
The common Schwinger functional Let D be the countable algebra generated by positive-time
translates of the bounded positive-scale cylinders and the normalized finite local source fields constructed
by the marked hierarchy. The multiscale source-tree theorem supplies a projective Schwinger functional
S on D with the following properties:
(S1) temperedness and continuity in every Schwartz test tensor;
(S2) stationarity, gauge invariance, permutation symmetry, and reflection positivity;
(S3) continuous Euclidean translations;
(S4) for every two field polynomials F, G with separated supports,

                              |S(F G) − S(F )S(G)| ≤ CF,G e−m dist1 (supp F,supp G)                   (166)

     with one m > 0 independent of the chosen polynomials;
(S5) the OS linear-growth estimate of Lemma 8.13 on every finite source multiplet.
The constants CF,G may depend on the fixed fields and test functions; the exponent m does not.
Theorem 9.1 (Continuous OS reconstruction). Let Θ be time reflection and D+ the positive-time
subalgebra. The form
                                        ⟨F, G⟩OS = S(ΘF G)
is positive semidefinite. Its quotient and completion give a separable Hilbert space H, vacuum Ω = [1],
and a strongly continuous self-adjoint contraction semigroup

                                         T (t) = e−tH ,    H ≥ 0.

Proof. Reflection positivity gives the positive form and the null ideal N = {F : ∥F ∥OS = 0}. Positive-time
translations preserve D+ for sufficiently small translations on each compactly supported word and define
contractions on the quotient; the usual time-zero limiting argument extends them to all t ≥ 0. For a core
vector [F ],
                                        2
                      ∥T (t)[F ] − [F ]∥ = S(ΘF τ2t F ) − 2ℜS(ΘF τt F ) + S(ΘF F ),
which tends to zero by translation continuity. Contractivity and density give strong continuity on H.
Reflection symmetry makes the semigroup self-adjoint, and the spectral theorem gives T (t) = e−tH with
H ≥ 0.

Spectral consequence of one common clustering rate Let D+,0 be the centered positive-time
core,
                              D+,0 = {F ∈ D+ : S(F ) = 0}.
For F ∈ D+,0 let µF be the positive spectral measure of H in the vector [F ].


                                                     89
Lemma 9.2 (Core spectral support). For every F ∈ D+,0 ,

                                              supp µF ⊂ [m, ∞).                                       (167)

Proof. Let the time support of F lie in a fixed compact interval. For all sufficiently large t, the supports
of ΘF and τt F are separated by at least t − cF . Since S(F ) = 0, (166) and OS reconstruction give
                               Z ∞
                          0≤        e−tλ dµF (λ) = ⟨[F ], T (t)[F ]⟩ ≤ CF e−mt .                       (168)
                                  0

Fix ε > 0. Then
                                      e−(m−ε)t µF ([0, m − ε]) ≤ CF e−mt .
Multiplication by e(m−ε)t and t → ∞ gives µF ([0, m − ε]) = 0. Letting ε ↓ 0 proves (167).

Theorem 9.3 (Unique vacuum and complete local mass gap). The OS Hamiltonian satisfies

                                               H Ω⊥ ≥ m > 0.                                          (169)

The vacuum is unique.
Proof. The centered classes [F ] − S(F )Ω, F ∈ D+ , are dense in Ω⊥ . Lemma 9.2 says that the spectral
projection EH ([0, m − ε]) annihilates this dense set for every ε > 0; hence it vanishes on Ω⊥ . This proves
the absence of spectrum in (0, m).
   For a centered core vector, (168) tends to zero, so its spectral measure has no atom at zero. Density
then shows that EH ({0})H = CΩ. Thus the vacuum is unique and (169) holds.

10     Nontriviality and finite spectral window
10.1     Endpoint interpolation and reflected noncollapse
Let Yr (f ) be the tangent-normalized, Wick-centered scalar curvature source at physical scale r, with the
source root parallel transported in the normalized finite-jet frame. The test profile f is chosen once at
unit scale and dilated with the source so that its dimensionless support remains fixed and lies strictly in
positive Euclidean time.
Lemma 10.1 (Positive Gaussian OS norm). There is such a profile f for which

                                    V0 (f ) := S0 ΘYr (f ) Yr (f ) > 0.
                                                                  


The value is independent of r in the normalized frame.
Proof. At the Gaussian fixed point the centered scalar curvature composite creates a two-curvature Fock
vector. In Fourier variables its squared norm is an integral of the squared symmetrized two-particle
amplitude against the positive transverse curvature spectral density. That density is nonzero on an open
set, so choosing a positive-time profile whose Fourier transform is nonzero there gives a strictly positive
integral. Reflection positivity identifies the integral with the displayed OS form. Scale covariance of the
tangent-normalized source makes the value independent of r.

Theorem 10.4 (Endpoint packet-shell supplier). For the graph-segment family constructed above, the
complete twice-marked endpoint expansion has the following properties.
    (i) The grounded periodic covariance has volume-independent AA, AF , and F F rows of orders two,
        three, and four. After the exact global contact is subtracted, every complete reflection/BRS packet
        has two lattice-difference factors and therefore gains (r/ℓi )2 at weak scale i.
   (ii) The exact weak recurrence admits a finite crossing-cell decomposition. On each fixed cell, both g
        derivatives are taken before the labelled large-field partition and before bad-core ownership. The
        differentiated partition is then recollected with the same charged events and with no same-owner
        cancellation assumption.
 (iii) Before crossing, the full rooted stable map, including wrapping and charged-flat coordinates, is C 2
        in g with a strictly contracting same-order marked row. At first crossing, the fresh marked family
        lies in a uniform hybrid pivot–jet polydisc and satisfies the original-link Fourier–Wiener incidence
        row required by the joint Haar–optical collect.
  (iv) The crossing contribution and the no-crossing terminal contribution are controlled by one geometri-
        cally summable conditional-packet shell majorant. The same majorant controls the functional and
        its first two g derivatives, extends to the closed crossing cells, and sends the terminal remainder to
        zero uniformly as the terminal depth tends to infinity.
Consequently the complete normalized two-root functional, with or without one fixed dimension-six defect
root, has a cutoff-, volume-, crossing-cell-, and terminal-depth-independent C 2 bound on 0 ≤ g ≤ gr .
Proof. Appendix A gives the full proof inside this manuscript. Its five subsections establish the grounded-
torus covariance and globally subtracted packet division; the weak crossing recurrence and wrapping rows;
the twice-marked conditional-packet contraction; the pre-owner hybrid-polydisc Wiener and compact-
entrance estimates; and the terminal-shell summation. No additional mathematical input is imported
beyond the results proved in Sections 3–7.

11     Completion for arbitrary fixed compact simple gauge groups
Theorem 11.1 (Completion of the fixed-group construction). For every fixed compact connected simple
G and faithful unitary representation ρ, the group register CG is finite and all hypotheses consumed by
Sections 2–10 are satisfied. Consequently Theorem 1.1 holds.
Proof. Compactness and the analytic bi-invariant geometry give the Karcher, logarithm, coarea, and
faithful-action constants of Section 2. The complete folded block symbol is spacetime-only and tensors
with Ig , so the strict right-inverse and optical coercivity constants are unchanged. The chart complement
is charged by the faithful Wilson action and the irregular activity is exponentially small. The permanent
weak map, centered graph, and BPHZL packet use the same finite-dimensional Lie-algebra tensor calculus,
with the universal color factor CA (G).
   The Peter–Weyl spectral sum converges at the fixed order sG , and the higher hybrid-polydisc argument
supplies the one-pivot nuclear and multipivot Fourier–Wiener rows. The ordinary-periodic toron, seam,
reflection, and wrapping templates are admitted by Theorem D.3; no twisted sector or finite-volume bundle
sum is used. The invariant-jet theorem supplies the complete low-dimensional mixing and dimension-six
regulator spaces. The joint Haar–optical forest, source transport, Ward hierarchy, OS reconstruction,
and noncollapse theorem then apply with constants depending on G but uniform in cutoff, volume, and
directed depth. Appendix F, specifically Table 3, records every independent entry of CG , the first proof
call at which its G-dependence enters, and the subsequent consumers. Thus no additional estimate
uniform in G is hidden in this completion step: after those finite calls, each remaining mechanism is
color-free, tensors with Ig , or takes a finite maximum in a registered finite-dimensional space.
Corollary 11.2 (The SU(N ) family). For every fixed integer N ≥ 2, the theorem holds for G = SU(N )
in the defining representation, and the scale-two map is
                                               11N log 2
                             u(2L) = u(L) +              u(L)2 + ON (u(L)3 ).

Remark 11.3 (Logical scope). The construction is a fixed-group theorem. The directed subsequence,
curvature-jet observable scope, and absence of a large-rank uniformity claim are part of the statement,
not omitted conclusions.

Finite incidence identities The finite clover and incidence formulas satisfy:
1. exact cancellation of the distinguished-link derivative in the two-plaquette clover;
2. exact gauge covariance under arbitrary lattice G gauge transformations;

4. exact signed-hypercubic covariance of the linear incidence stencil;
5. the zero trace of every first nonlinear adjoint diagonal block.
These identities, together with the all-stage marked theorem and the terminal joint Haar–optical connector
established in Part III, complete the finite-cutoff source construction.

(S1) linear and Schwartz tempered in f ;
(S2) multilinear and continuous in bounded exterior cylinders;

complete filtered low-jet block is normalized by Theorem 8.24; the remaining antisymmetric regulator
response is two engineering dimensions higher after one or two geometric marks. The angular Ward
theorem below then integrates the resulting infinitesimal action to O(4) covariance.

Proposition 8.36 (Noncollapse and canonical normalization). The stress Schwinger distribution is
nonzero. Its normalization is fixed uniquely by the unit contact coefficient in (160); no independent finite
factor is available.
Proof. The reflected curvature source of Theorem 10.5 (proved independently in Section 10) defines

finite positive spectral measure supported away from zero; hence that correlation is not constant and its
distributional translation derivative is nonzero for suitable separated test profiles. Choose v supported
near one insertion. The right side of (160) is then nonzero, so the tensor insertion on the left cannot
vanish. Multiplying the tensor by a finite constant would multiply the left contact coefficient but not the
geometric derivation on the right, forcing the constant to be one.

Theorem 8.37 (Stress/Ward construction). The global displacement theorem plus the projective construc-
tion above produce a nonzero tempered Euclidean tensor insertion on the bounded geometric Schwinger
algebra, with exact translation contacts and signed-hypercubic covariance. The later angular Ward theorem
promotes its continuum multiplet to O(4) covariance.
8.2.5 Euclidean rotation covariance
Low-dimensional rotation-defect classification.
Lemma 8.38 (No low-dimensional gauge-invariant vector). For every compact simple G there is no
nonzero local, reflection-compatible, gauge-invariant vector source of engineering dimension at most four,
modulo vacuum terms and total derivatives.
Proof. A connection mass term is not gauge invariant. At dimension two, Tr dρ(Fµν ) = 0 by semisimplicity.
At dimension three, traces of dρ(DF ) reduce to derivatives of Tr dρ(F ) and vanish. A dimension-four
gauge-invariant vector cannot be formed from two curvature two-forms using only the metric while leaving
one free index; an epsilon tensor would have the wrong reflection parity. Total derivatives are excluded
by the stated quotient.


                                                         86
Lemma 8.39 (Antisymmetric stress defect begins at dimension six). A local signed-hypercubic, reflection-
even, gauge-invariant antisymmetric rank-two source has no component of engineering dimension four or
five. Its first possible nonzero continuum jet has dimension at least six.
Proof. At dimension four the quadratic curvature tensors are generated by Tr(Fµα Fνα ), δµν Tr F 2 , and
the corresponding dual-curvature contractions. The first is symmetric; the dual contraction is proportional
to a diagonal pseudoscalar tensor; none has an antisymmetric part. Reflection symmetry excludes the
odd-derivative dimension-five sector.
Corollary 8.40 (Vanishing rotation regulator). In the projective stress Ward identity, the antisymmetric
regulator insertion is a2 times a normalized family of dimension-six roots. The marked source bounds
therefore make it vanish in the zero-spacing limit. The cutoff rotation Ward identities integrate to full O(4)
covariance once the already established translation, reflection, and tensor-source continuity statements are
inserted.

Finite source multiplets and integration For every D < ∞, let J≤D be the finite-dimensional
renormalized curvature-jet source multiplet. The contact hierarchy preserves the engineering filtration.
On J≤D , δω is a finite matrix, allowing same-dimension composite-operator mixing.
Lemma 8.44 (Rotation algebra on finite source multiplets). The contact derivations obey

                                               [δω , δη ] = δ[ω,η]

on every J≤D and are compatible under D 7→ D + 2.
Proof. At finite cutoff the contact derivations are obtained by differentiating the geometric pullback of test
profiles, tensor indices and local source words. Geometric Lie derivatives obey the displayed commutator
identity exactly. Finite triangular source renormalization conjugates this finite-dimensional action and
therefore preserves the commutator. Dimension-raised regulator contacts vanish by Lemma 8.41; the
projective limit and the inclusions of source multiplets preserve the identity.
   Every finite-dimensional real representation of so(4) integrates to the simply connected group Spin(4).
The source multiplets consist of bosonic tensors built from curvature and covariant derivatives, so a 2π
rotation acts trivially; equivalently, their geometric tensor action factors through SO(4).
Theorem 8.45 (SO(4) covariance of the Schwinger family). Let ρD be the integrated finite-dimensional
action on J≤D . Then for every R ∈ SO(4),

                                  Sn (ρD (R)v1 , Rf1 ), . . . , (ρD (R)vn , Rfn )
                                                                                 

                                        = Sn (v1 , f1 ), . . . , (vn , fn ) .
                                                                           


Exact lattice reflection extends the covariance to O(4).
Proof. Along a smooth path R(s) in SO(4), differentiate the transformed Schwinger function. The
derivative is the left side of (165) with ω = R′ (s)R(s)−1 and therefore vanishes. Lemma 8.44 gives path
independence and the group law. Reflection is exact for the Wilson action, block map, clover source and
finite source normalization.



                                                       88
Osterwalder–Schrader continuation Let Dloc be the local field-polynomial test algebra. The O(4)
covariance proved above is an identity of the Euclidean Schwinger system. Rotations preserving the
chosen time-zero hyperplane act directly on the positive-time quotient; rotations mixing Euclidean time
with space need not preserve that quotient and are not asserted to act unitarily there.
Theorem 8.46 (Euclidean covariance and Poincaré reconstruction). The Schwinger family satisfies the
full O(4) covariance axiom. Together with reflection positivity, permutation symmetry, temperedness
and continuous translations, this gives by the Osterwalder–Schrader reconstruction theorem a strongly
continuous unitary representation of the proper orthochronous Poincaré group on the reconstructed Hilbert
space, with translation spectrum in the closed forward cone. Spatial rotations and translations are obtained
directly on the OS quotient; Lorentz boosts arise from analytic continuation of the Euclidean rotation
covariance.
Proof. Theorem 8.45 is precisely the Euclidean covariance identity required for every finite tensor/source
multiplet, and the nested source construction extends it to the complete chosen local field family. The
direct OS theorem, Lemma 8.14, and Lemma 8.13 supply the remaining reconstruction hypotheses,
including the linear-growth condition. The standard Osterwalder–Schrader covariance reconstruction
then produces the positive- energy Poincaré representation. No unitary action of a Euclidean rotation
that changes the reflection hyperplane is assumed on the intermediate OS quotient.

9     Continuum reconstruction and spectral gap
9.1    Osterwalder–Schrader reconstruction and clustering gap
The common Schwinger functional Let D be the countable algebra generated by positive-time
translates of the bounded positive-scale cylinders and the normalized finite local source fields constructed
by the marked hierarchy. The multiscale source-tree theorem supplies a projective Schwinger functional
S on D with the following properties:
(S1) temperedness and continuity in every Schwartz test tensor;
(S2) stationarity, gauge invariance, permutation symmetry, and reflection positivity;
(S3) continuous Euclidean translations;
(S4) for every two field polynomials F, G with separated supports,

                              |S(F G) − S(F )S(G)| ≤ CF,G e−m dist1 (supp F,supp G)                   (166)

     with one m > 0 independent of the chosen polynomials;
(S5) the OS linear-growth estimate of Lemma 8.13 on every finite source multiplet.
The constants CF,G may depend on the fixed fields and test functions; the exponent m does not.
Theorem 9.1 (Continuous OS reconstruction). Let Θ be time reflection and D+ the positive-time
subalgebra. The form
                                        ⟨F, G⟩OS = S(ΘF G)
is positive semidefinite. Its quotient and completion give a separable Hilbert space H, vacuum Ω = [1],
and a strongly continuous self-adjoint contraction semigroup

                                         T (t) = e−tH ,    H ≥ 0.

Proof. Reflection positivity gives the positive form and the null ideal N = {F : ∥F ∥OS = 0}. Positive-time
translations preserve D+ for sufficiently small translations on each compactly supported word and define
contractions on the quotient; the usual time-zero limiting argument extends them to all t ≥ 0. For a core
vector [F ],
                                        2
                      ∥T (t)[F ] − [F ]∥ = S(ΘF τ2t F ) − 2ℜS(ΘF τt F ) + S(ΘF F ),
which tends to zero by translation continuity. Contractivity and density give strong continuity on H.
Reflection symmetry makes the semigroup self-adjoint, and the spectral theorem gives T (t) = e−tH with
H ≥ 0.

Spectral consequence of one common clustering rate Let D+,0 be the centered positive-time
core,
                              D+,0 = {F ∈ D+ : S(F ) = 0}.
For F ∈ D+,0 let µF be the positive spectral measure of H in the vector [F ].


                                                     89
Lemma 9.2 (Core spectral support). For every F ∈ D+,0 ,

                                              supp µF ⊂ [m, ∞).                                       (167)

Proof. Let the time support of F lie in a fixed compact interval. For all sufficiently large t, the supports
of ΘF and τt F are separated by at least t − cF . Since S(F ) = 0, (166) and OS reconstruction give
                               Z ∞
                          0≤        e−tλ dµF (λ) = ⟨[F ], T (t)[F ]⟩ ≤ CF e−mt .                       (168)
                                  0

Fix ε > 0. Then
                                      e−(m−ε)t µF ([0, m − ε]) ≤ CF e−mt .
Multiplication by e(m−ε)t and t → ∞ gives µF ([0, m − ε]) = 0. Letting ε ↓ 0 proves (167).

Theorem 9.3 (Unique vacuum and complete local mass gap). The OS Hamiltonian satisfies

                                               H Ω⊥ ≥ m > 0.                                          (169)

The vacuum is unique.
Proof. The centered classes [F ] − S(F )Ω, F ∈ D+ , are dense in Ω⊥ . Lemma 9.2 says that the spectral
projection EH ([0, m − ε]) annihilates this dense set for every ε > 0; hence it vanishes on Ω⊥ . This proves
the absence of spectrum in (0, m).
   For a centered core vector, (168) tends to zero, so its spectral measure has no atom at zero. Density
then shows that EH ({0})H = CΩ. Thus the vacuum is unique and (169) holds.

10     Nontriviality and finite spectral window
10.1     Endpoint interpolation and reflected noncollapse
Let Yr (f ) be the tangent-normalized, Wick-centered scalar curvature source at physical scale r, with the
source root parallel transported in the normalized finite-jet frame. The test profile f is chosen once at
unit scale and dilated with the source so that its dimensionless support remains fixed and lies strictly in
positive Euclidean time.
Lemma 10.1 (Positive Gaussian OS norm). There is such a profile f for which

                                    V0 (f ) := S0 ΘYr (f ) Yr (f ) > 0.
                                                                  


The value is independent of r in the normalized frame.
Proof. At the Gaussian fixed point the centered scalar curvature composite creates a two-curvature Fock
vector. In Fourier variables its squared norm is an integral of the squared symmetrized two-particle
amplitude against the positive transverse curvature spectral density. That density is nonzero on an open
set, so choosing a positive-time profile whose Fourier transform is nonzero there gives a strictly positive
integral. Reflection positivity identifies the integral with the displayed OS form. Scale covariance of the
tangent-normalized source makes the value independent of r.

10.2     Uniform endpoint interpolation

        and terminal depth M ;
  (iii) differentiation is performed inside each fixed crossing cell before ownership, never on the crossing

  (iv) the no-crossing terminal remainder tends to zero through two g-derivatives uniformly as M → ∞.

Theorem 10.4 (Endpoint packet-shell supplier). For the graph-segment family constructed above, the
complete twice-marked endpoint expansion has the following properties.
    (i) The grounded periodic covariance has volume-independent AA, AF , and F F rows of orders two,
        three, and four. After the exact global contact is subtracted, every complete reflection/BRS packet
        has two lattice-difference factors and therefore gains (r/ℓi )2 at weak scale i.
   (ii) The exact weak recurrence admits a finite crossing-cell decomposition. On each fixed cell, both g
        derivatives are taken before the labelled large-field partition and before bad-core ownership. The
        differentiated partition is then recollected with the same charged events and with no same-owner
        cancellation assumption.
 (iii) Before crossing, the full rooted stable map, including wrapping and charged-flat coordinates, is C 2
        in g with a strictly contracting same-order marked row. At first crossing, the fresh marked family
        lies in a uniform hybrid pivot–jet polydisc and satisfies the original-link Fourier–Wiener incidence
        row required by the joint Haar–optical collect.
  (iv) The crossing contribution and the no-crossing terminal contribution are controlled by one geometri-
        cally summable conditional-packet shell majorant. The same majorant controls the functional and
        its first two g derivatives, extends to the closed crossing cells, and sends the terminal remainder to
        zero uniformly as the terminal depth tends to infinity.
Consequently the complete normalized two-root functional, with or without one fixed dimension-six defect
root, has a cutoff-, volume-, crossing-cell-, and terminal-depth-independent C 2 bound on 0 ≤ g ≤ gr .
Proof. Appendix A gives the full proof inside this manuscript. Its five subsections establish the grounded-
torus covariance and globally subtracted packet division; the weak crossing recurrence and wrapping rows;
the twice-marked conditional-packet contraction; the pre-owner hybrid-polydisc Wiener and compact-
entrance estimates; and the terminal-shell summation. No additional mathematical input is imported
beyond the results proved in Sections 3–7.

11     Completion for arbitrary fixed compact simple gauge groups
Theorem 11.1 (Completion of the fixed-group construction). For every fixed compact connected simple
G and faithful unitary representation ρ, the group register CG is finite and all hypotheses consumed by
Sections 2–10 are satisfied. Consequently Theorem 1.1 holds.
Proof. Compactness and the analytic bi-invariant geometry give the Karcher, logarithm, coarea, and
faithful-action constants of Section 2. The complete folded block symbol is spacetime-only and tensors
with Ig , so the strict right-inverse and optical coercivity constants are unchanged. The chart complement
is charged by the faithful Wilson action and the irregular activity is exponentially small. The permanent
weak map, centered graph, and BPHZL packet use the same finite-dimensional Lie-algebra tensor calculus,
with the universal color factor CA (G).
   The Peter–Weyl spectral sum converges at the fixed order sG , and the higher hybrid-polydisc argument
supplies the one-pivot nuclear and multipivot Fourier–Wiener rows. The ordinary-periodic toron, seam,
reflection, and wrapping templates are admitted by Theorem D.3; no twisted sector or finite-volume bundle
sum is used. The invariant-jet theorem supplies the complete low-dimensional mixing and dimension-six
regulator spaces. The joint Haar–optical forest, source transport, Ward hierarchy, OS reconstruction,
and noncollapse theorem then apply with constants depending on G but uniform in cutoff, volume, and
directed depth. Appendix F, specifically Table 3, records every independent entry of CG , the first proof
call at which its G-dependence enters, and the subsequent consumers. Thus no additional estimate
uniform in G is hidden in this completion step: after those finite calls, each remaining mechanism is
color-free, tensors with Ig , or takes a finite maximum in a registered finite-dimensional space.
Corollary 11.2 (The SU(N ) family). For every fixed integer N ≥ 2, the theorem holds for G = SU(N )
in the defining representation, and the scale-two map is
                                               11N log 2
                             u(2L) = u(L) +              u(L)2 + ON (u(L)3 ).
                                                 24π 2
No estimate uniform as N → ∞ is asserted.
Remark 11.3 (Logical scope). The construction is a fixed-group theorem. The directed subsequence,
curvature-jet observable scope, and absence of a large-rank uniformity claim are part of the statement,
not omitted conclusions.

A     Endpoint packet-shell verification
This appendix supplies the detailed proof of Theorem 10.4. It is part of the same manuscript and
uses the standing uniformity convention of Section 1. The main text isolates the logical endpoint
theorem; the statements below retain the full finite-volume, crossing-cell, pre-owner, compact-entrance,
and terminal-shell verification needed for that theorem.

theorem below then integrates the resulting infinitesimal action to O(4) covariance.

Proposition 8.36 (Noncollapse and canonical normalization). The stress Schwinger distribution is
nonzero. Its normalization is fixed uniquely by the unit contact coefficient in (160); no independent finite
factor is available.
Proof. The reflected curvature source of Theorem 10.5 (proved independently in Section 10) defines

finite positive spectral measure supported away from zero; hence that correlation is not constant and its
distributional translation derivative is nonzero for suitable separated test profiles. Choose v supported
near one insertion. The right side of (160) is then nonzero, so the tensor insertion on the left cannot
vanish. Multiplying the tensor by a finite constant would multiply the left contact coefficient but not the
geometric derivation on the right, forcing the constant to be one.

Theorem 8.37 (Stress/Ward construction). The global displacement theorem plus the projective construc-
tion above produce a nonzero tempered Euclidean tensor insertion on the bounded geometric Schwinger
algebra, with exact translation contacts and signed-hypercubic covariance. The later angular Ward theorem
promotes its continuum multiplet to O(4) covariance.
8.2.5 Euclidean rotation covariance
Low-dimensional rotation-defect classification.
Lemma 8.38 (No low-dimensional gauge-invariant vector). For every compact simple G there is no
nonzero local, reflection-compatible, gauge-invariant vector source of engineering dimension at most four,
modulo vacuum terms and total derivatives.
Proof. A connection mass term is not gauge invariant. At dimension two, Tr dρ(Fµν ) = 0 by semisimplicity.
At dimension three, traces of dρ(DF ) reduce to derivatives of Tr dρ(F ) and vanish. A dimension-four
gauge-invariant vector cannot be formed from two curvature two-forms using only the metric while leaving
one free index; an epsilon tensor would have the wrong reflection parity. Total derivatives are excluded
by the stated quotient.


                                                         86
Lemma 8.39 (Antisymmetric stress defect begins at dimension six). A local signed-hypercubic, reflection-
even, gauge-invariant antisymmetric rank-two source has no component of engineering dimension four or
five. Its first possible nonzero continuum jet has dimension at least six.
Proof. At dimension four the quadratic curvature tensors are generated by Tr(Fµα Fνα ), δµν Tr F 2 , and
the corresponding dual-curvature contractions. The first is symmetric; the dual contraction is proportional
to a diagonal pseudoscalar tensor; none has an antisymmetric part. Reflection symmetry excludes the
odd-derivative dimension-five sector.
Corollary 8.40 (Vanishing rotation regulator). In the projective stress Ward identity, the antisymmetric
regulator insertion is a2 times a normalized family of dimension-six roots. The marked source bounds
therefore make it vanish in the zero-spacing limit. The cutoff rotation Ward identities integrate to full O(4)
covariance once the already established translation, reflection, and tensor-source continuity statements are
inserted.

Finite source multiplets and integration For every D < ∞, let J≤D be the finite-dimensional
renormalized curvature-jet source multiplet. The contact hierarchy preserves the engineering filtration.
On J≤D , δω is a finite matrix, allowing same-dimension composite-operator mixing.
Lemma 8.44 (Rotation algebra on finite source multiplets). The contact derivations obey

                                               [δω , δη ] = δ[ω,η]

on every J≤D and are compatible under D 7→ D + 2.
Proof. At finite cutoff the contact derivations are obtained by differentiating the geometric pullback of test
profiles, tensor indices and local source words. Geometric Lie derivatives obey the displayed commutator
identity exactly. Finite triangular source renormalization conjugates this finite-dimensional action and
therefore preserves the commutator. Dimension-raised regulator contacts vanish by Lemma 8.41; the
projective limit and the inclusions of source multiplets preserve the identity.
   Every finite-dimensional real representation of so(4) integrates to the simply connected group Spin(4).
The source multiplets consist of bosonic tensors built from curvature and covariant derivatives, so a 2π
rotation acts trivially; equivalently, their geometric tensor action factors through SO(4).
Theorem 8.45 (SO(4) covariance of the Schwinger family). Let ρD be the integrated finite-dimensional
action on J≤D . Then for every R ∈ SO(4),

                                  Sn (ρD (R)v1 , Rf1 ), . . . , (ρD (R)vn , Rfn )
                                                                                 

                                        = Sn (v1 , f1 ), . . . , (vn , fn ) .
                                                                           


Exact lattice reflection extends the covariance to O(4).
Proof. Along a smooth path R(s) in SO(4), differentiate the transformed Schwinger function. The
derivative is the left side of (165) with ω = R′ (s)R(s)−1 and therefore vanishes. Lemma 8.44 gives path
independence and the group law. Reflection is exact for the Wilson action, block map, clover source and
finite source normalization.



                                                       88
Osterwalder–Schrader continuation Let Dloc be the local field-polynomial test algebra. The O(4)
covariance proved above is an identity of the Euclidean Schwinger system. Rotations preserving the
chosen time-zero hyperplane act directly on the positive-time quotient; rotations mixing Euclidean time
with space need not preserve that quotient and are not asserted to act unitarily there.
Theorem 8.46 (Euclidean covariance and Poincaré reconstruction). The Schwinger family satisfies the
full O(4) covariance axiom. Together with reflection positivity, permutation symmetry, temperedness
and continuous translations, this gives by the Osterwalder–Schrader reconstruction theorem a strongly
continuous unitary representation of the proper orthochronous Poincaré group on the reconstructed Hilbert
space, with translation spectrum in the closed forward cone. Spatial rotations and translations are obtained
directly on the OS quotient; Lorentz boosts arise from analytic continuation of the Euclidean rotation
covariance.
Proof. Theorem 8.45 is precisely the Euclidean covariance identity required for every finite tensor/source
multiplet, and the nested source construction extends it to the complete chosen local field family. The
direct OS theorem, Lemma 8.14, and Lemma 8.13 supply the remaining reconstruction hypotheses,
including the linear-growth condition. The standard Osterwalder–Schrader covariance reconstruction
then produces the positive- energy Poincaré representation. No unitary action of a Euclidean rotation
that changes the reflection hyperplane is assumed on the intermediate OS quotient.

9     Continuum reconstruction and spectral gap
9.1    Osterwalder–Schrader reconstruction and clustering gap
The common Schwinger functional Let D be the countable algebra generated by positive-time
translates of the bounded positive-scale cylinders and the normalized finite local source fields constructed
by the marked hierarchy. The multiscale source-tree theorem supplies a projective Schwinger functional
S on D with the following properties:
(S1) temperedness and continuity in every Schwartz test tensor;
(S2) stationarity, gauge invariance, permutation symmetry, and reflection positivity;
(S3) continuous Euclidean translations;
(S4) for every two field polynomials F, G with separated supports,

                              |S(F G) − S(F )S(G)| ≤ CF,G e−m dist1 (supp F,supp G)                   (166)

     with one m > 0 independent of the chosen polynomials;
(S5) the OS linear-growth estimate of Lemma 8.13 on every finite source multiplet.
The constants CF,G may depend on the fixed fields and test functions; the exponent m does not.
Theorem 9.1 (Continuous OS reconstruction). Let Θ be time reflection and D+ the positive-time
subalgebra. The form
                                        ⟨F, G⟩OS = S(ΘF G)
is positive semidefinite. Its quotient and completion give a separable Hilbert space H, vacuum Ω = [1],
and a strongly continuous self-adjoint contraction semigroup

                                         T (t) = e−tH ,    H ≥ 0.

Proof. Reflection positivity gives the positive form and the null ideal N = {F : ∥F ∥OS = 0}. Positive-time
translations preserve D+ for sufficiently small translations on each compactly supported word and define
contractions on the quotient; the usual time-zero limiting argument extends them to all t ≥ 0. For a core
vector [F ],
                                        2
                      ∥T (t)[F ] − [F ]∥ = S(ΘF τ2t F ) − 2ℜS(ΘF τt F ) + S(ΘF F ),
which tends to zero by translation continuity. Contractivity and density give strong continuity on H.
Reflection symmetry makes the semigroup self-adjoint, and the spectral theorem gives T (t) = e−tH with
H ≥ 0.

Spectral consequence of one common clustering rate Let D+,0 be the centered positive-time
core,
                              D+,0 = {F ∈ D+ : S(F ) = 0}.
For F ∈ D+,0 let µF be the positive spectral measure of H in the vector [F ].


                                                     89
Lemma 9.2 (Core spectral support). For every F ∈ D+,0 ,

                                              supp µF ⊂ [m, ∞).                                       (167)

Proof. Let the time support of F lie in a fixed compact interval. For all sufficiently large t, the supports
of ΘF and τt F are separated by at least t − cF . Since S(F ) = 0, (166) and OS reconstruction give
                               Z ∞
                          0≤        e−tλ dµF (λ) = ⟨[F ], T (t)[F ]⟩ ≤ CF e−mt .                       (168)
                                  0

Fix ε > 0. Then
                                      e−(m−ε)t µF ([0, m − ε]) ≤ CF e−mt .
Multiplication by e(m−ε)t and t → ∞ gives µF ([0, m − ε]) = 0. Letting ε ↓ 0 proves (167).

Theorem 9.3 (Unique vacuum and complete local mass gap). The OS Hamiltonian satisfies

                                               H Ω⊥ ≥ m > 0.                                          (169)

The vacuum is unique.
Proof. The centered classes [F ] − S(F )Ω, F ∈ D+ , are dense in Ω⊥ . Lemma 9.2 says that the spectral
projection EH ([0, m − ε]) annihilates this dense set for every ε > 0; hence it vanishes on Ω⊥ . This proves
the absence of spectrum in (0, m).
   For a centered core vector, (168) tends to zero, so its spectral measure has no atom at zero. Density
then shows that EH ({0})H = CΩ. Thus the vacuum is unique and (169) holds.

10     Nontriviality and finite spectral window
10.1     Endpoint interpolation and reflected noncollapse
Let Yr (f ) be the tangent-normalized, Wick-centered scalar curvature source at physical scale r, with the
source root parallel transported in the normalized finite-jet frame. The test profile f is chosen once at
unit scale and dilated with the source so that its dimensionless support remains fixed and lies strictly in
positive Euclidean time.
Lemma 10.1 (Positive Gaussian OS norm). There is such a profile f for which

                                    V0 (f ) := S0 ΘYr (f ) Yr (f ) > 0.
                                                                  


The value is independent of r in the normalized frame.
Proof. At the Gaussian fixed point the centered scalar curvature composite creates a two-curvature Fock
vector. In Fourier variables its squared norm is an integral of the squared symmetrized two-particle
amplitude against the positive transverse curvature spectral density. That density is nonzero on an open
set, so choosing a positive-time profile whose Fourier transform is nonzero there gives a strictly positive
integral. Reflection positivity identifies the integral with the displayed OS form. Scale covariance of the
tangent-normalized source makes the value independent of r.

Theorem 10.4 (Endpoint packet-shell supplier). For the graph-segment family constructed above, the

    (i) The grounded periodic covariance has volume-independent AA, AF , and F F rows of orders two,

        derivatives are taken before the labelled large-field partition and before bad-core ownership. The
        differentiated partition is then recollected with the same charged events and with no same-owner
        cancellation assumption.
 (iii) Before crossing, the full rooted stable map, including wrapping and charged-flat coordinates, is C 2
        in g with a strictly contracting same-order marked row. At first crossing, the fresh marked family
        lies in a uniform hybrid pivot–jet polydisc and satisfies the original-link Fourier–Wiener incidence
        row required by the joint Haar–optical collect.
  (iv) The crossing contribution and the no-crossing terminal contribution are controlled by one geometri-
        cally summable conditional-packet shell majorant. The same majorant controls the functional and
        its first two g derivatives, extends to the closed crossing cells, and sends the terminal remainder to
        zero uniformly as the terminal depth tends to infinity.

root, has a cutoff-, volume-, crossing-cell-, and terminal-depth-independent C 2 bound on 0 ≤ g ≤ gr .
